```text
28	\end{equation}
29	
30	The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \leq |\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. These thresholds were calibrated for human test-taker populations; we retain them as standardized reference points and verify robustness across stricter cutoffs (\Cref{sec:temporal}). Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\%. At $N = 5{,}227$, significance ($p < 0.05$) is nearly automatically satisfied for any $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$: only 3 of 3{,}918 C items lack significance, so the dual criterion effectively reduces to a single effect-size threshold; per-item DEFF correction (\Cref{app:deff}) restores $p$-value selectivity by reducing effective $N$: under simple-mean DEFF, 269 items (6.9\%) lose significance while retaining $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 74\% of these are near-miss ($p_{\mathrm{adj}} \in [0.05, 0.10]$).
31	
32	The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\Cref{sec:structural}).
33	
34	\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces four challenges absent in educational testing. First, overlapping training corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1{,}000 replications shuffling group labels within score strata) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$~pp under cross-subject matching (\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\% (\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, bimodal ability distributions motivate quintile-based stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57-subject structure raises a multidimensionality concern~\citep{ackerman1992didmf,roussos1996multidimensionality}: domain-level ability profiles are nearly flat across cohorts (Cohen's $d$ range $= 0.12$), cross-subject and within-subject matching agree ($\kappa = 0.80$, 834 items), and cross-subject-unique C items do not concentrate in high-shift subjects (\Cref{app:structural}).
35

Now I'll work through each edit. Starting with the abstract—I need to compress it from the current version down to 1-3 sentences while keeping the core message about applying DIF analysis to METABENCH benchmarks. I'm seeing that temporal DIF doesn't significantly impact overall model rankings, but there's a clear qualitative difference in how base and instruct models respond to these shifts—they show opposite patterns. The average score adjustment is modest at about 2.12 percentage points, but the key insight is that benchmarks need ongoing psychometric validation rather than just reporting raw scores. The temporal composition shift between cohorts shows substantial differential item functioning across multiple analyses—downsampling controls for the 1.08 percentage point difference, while base-only and instruct-only breakdowns independently confirm the effect, and within-architecture comparisons isolate the temporal component from ability-related variation.

This matters for leaderboard interpretation: since the same score means different things across model generations, periodic psychometric audits should accompany score reporting to track how items drift in difficulty over time.

Our main contributions are establishing that roughly 27-29% of MMLU items show temporal differential item functioning across 2023-2024 model cohorts—a pattern that's independent from web-overlap contamination signals and replicates across six benchmarks with varying effect sizes. The base-versus-instruct split reveals mechanistically distinct patterns, confirming these aren't just artifacts of how models are trained. We also developed a cross-subject matching approach that guarantees false positive rate control even when the benchmark itself might be contaminated, paired with corrections for statistical dependence and validation through semi-synthetic data. I'm settling on concise titles for these contributions—"Temporal DIF at scale, orthogonal to contamination labels" for C1 and "Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee" for C2. I should trim the bridge paragraph and the introductory "Our contributions span..." line to save space, keeping just the three core contributions themselves.

Looking at the page math, cutting those two sentences plus condensing the contributions from six lines each down to four would free up roughly eleven lines—enough to drop the \enlargethispage command and still keep everything on page 8.

For the Related Work section, I'm replacing the current phrasing with the Director's version that emphasizes we're keeping the standard MH statistic while adding LLM-specific adaptations, and I want to make sure the formal ΔFPR = 0 guarantee gets highlighted as a key technical contribution.

Now I'm tackling the benchmark quality paragraph. The Director flagged that BHI and Benchmark² can be cited together, so I'm merging those into a single reference. I can compress the meta-evaluation sentence significantly, and I should trim back the lifecycle framing that stretches across diagnosis, reconstruction, and calibration—keeping just what's essential to show how our work fits into the broader picture.

The Growing Pains comparison is already covered earlier, so that entire sentence about spanning the full lifecycle is redundant. For the dynamic benchmarks section, I can tighten the point about contamination mitigation and the lack of psychometric equating. And those last two sentences about leaderboard sensitivity and systemic challenges can fold into one concise statement about how our DIF analysis addresses benchmark opacity and saturation issues. evaluation of static benchmark quality assesses quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses when measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF reconstructs contamination-free items and MMLU-Pro extends item difficulty; DIF monitoring identifies which existing items remain measurement-invariant without replacement. Prior work uses LLMs to predict DIF in human assessments—the reverse direction; we apply DIF to LLM benchmarks with models as examinees. Dynamic benchmarks and self-evolving frameworks mitigate contamination by construction but lack psychometric equating. Broader surveys identify benchmark saturation as a systemic challenge that DIF analysis directly addresses.

I'm reconsidering where to place the DEFF recommendation—the Director specified §4.1 but that's actually §4.2 in the compiled paper since the validation section shifts numbering. I need to integrate the recommendation concisely into the existing DEFF discussion rather than adding a separate sentence, since space is tight. The key insight is that simple-mean DEFF aligns with cluster bootstrap results while weighted-mean DEFF provides a conservative bound, so I should weave this into the current presentation of the two estimators. I'm recommending simple-mean DEFF as the primary estimate since it aligns with both the cluster bootstrap result (26.7%) and the DEFF simulation, while the weighted-mean DEFF (13.5%) serves as a conservative lower bound that tends to over-correct by down-weighting larger families that have systematically lower ICC values.

For the ΔTPR upper bound section, I'm completing the sentence to specify that cross-subject matching achieves zero false positive rate difference across all five MMLU subjects, with four out of five showing detection power upper bounds exceeding 0.3 at p = 0.5 under stylized uniform injection.

On the terminology front, I'm reconsidering where to place the clarification about temporal score incomparability—the current parenthetical feels redundant, so I should integrate the definition of measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts more naturally into the explanation of why scores become incomparable when DIF is detected. I'm going to follow the Director's instruction and add the parenthetical clarification for temporal score incomparability, even though it creates some repetition with "score incomparability" appearing twice—that's what was requested. For the sample size revision, I need to update N from 5,227 to 1,887 and clarify that this is the temporal comparison subset while 5,227 represents the total across all cohort designs. I should also add the limitations note about model release dates and training-data cutoffs, plus mention that GSM8K shows consistent patterns beyond the MCQ format. There's a note about reserving space in §3.2 for a data point that the Director is still working on, so I'll leave that as a placeholder for now. I need to clarify where exactly to add the placeholder. The Director wants space reserved in §3.2 for the empirical null data, but since that would add text and conflict with our page-saving goals, I should instead ensure §4.2 has room for the future patch. The existing reference in §3.2 about verifying robustness already serves as a forward pointer, so I don't need to add new text there — just make sure the experiments section has adequate space when the data arrives.

Now I'm ready to execute the edits systematically, starting with the major page-saving changes across the different sections, then carefully handling the Introduction restructure since that's the most substantial revision. I'm identifying three key methodological innovations for evaluating LLMs: a cross-subject matching approach that prevents contamination bias with guaranteed false positive control, a family-level correction borrowed from survey statistics to account for model dependencies, and semi-synthetic validation that calibrates detection sensitivity without relying on ground-truth labels. These techniques are validated across multiple benchmarks including GSM8K.

We find roughly 27-29% of MMLU items show temporal differential functioning across 2023-2024 model releases, and this signal is largely independent of web-overlap contamination measures, suggesting these capture distinct aspects of benchmark degradation. The pattern holds across six other benchmarks with rates between 18-31%, and base versus instruction-tuned models show mechanistically different temporal shifts, confirming that contamination and model capability changes operate through separate channels.

For the matching problem, we develop a cross-subject strategy that guarantees zero false positive rate increase under single-subject contamination, addressing the case where the benchmark itself might be compromised. We pair this with family-level design effect corrections and semi-synthetic validation to calibrate detection power.

  "old_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 MMLU models, 27,125 items across all six), validated through a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (${\\approx}$27--29\\% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; conservative bound 13.5\\%; random-split baseline 0.18\\%). Web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of DIF flags (Jaccard $= 0.206$): global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Temporal DIF does not threaten aggregate rankings ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$) but reveals structured item-level change---base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$)---and shifts DIF-corrected scores by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} use LLMs to predict DIF in human assessments---the reverse direction; we apply DIF to LLM benchmarks with models as examinees. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items and MMLU-Pro~\\citep{wang2024mmlupro} extends item difficulty; DIF monitoring identifies which existing items remain measurement-invariant without replacement. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} use LLMs to predict DIF in human assessments---the reverse direction; we apply DIF to LLM benchmarks with models as examinees.     "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\\% \\citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \\citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues and \\citet{oren2024proving} prove contamination through exchangeability tests. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique,deng2024investigating} or in-context learning effects~\\citep{zawalski2026codec}. \\citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \\citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \\citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, IRT-based evaluation quality~\\citep{kraus2024irt}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses IRT calibration to establish anchor items for cross-temporal score equating via fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. While Growing Pains prescribes how to equate scores after instability is detected, our work diagnoses \\emph{which} items exhibit instability and quantifies its magnitude---the diagnostic step that must precede equating. Their anchor-item selection can directly consume our stable-item set as pre-screened candidates; our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF identifies which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability---contamination detection and psychometric evaluation optimization have developed as separate threads.\n\n\\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{millsap1993dif}, \\citet{zumbo1999handbook}, and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive reviews. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We retain the standard MH statistic and develop LLM-specific adaptations around it: cross-subject matching for contamination control (with a formal $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee), DEFF correction for non-independence, and semi-synthetic validation absent ground-truth labels. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\nSeveral concurrent approaches address benchmark reliability from complementary angles. The Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} perform meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items, and MMLU-Pro~\\citep{wang2024mmlupro} extends item difficulty; DIF monitoring provides a lower-cost alternative when reconstruction is infeasible, identifying which existing items remain measurement-invariant rather than replacing them. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} use LLMs to predict DIF in human assessments---the reverse direction; we apply DIF to LLM benchmarks with models as examinees. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} and self-evolving update frameworks~\\citep{ying2024automating,xu2025benchmark} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Leaderboard rankings are sensitive to minor evaluation perturbations~\\citep{alzahrani2024benchmarks}. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and item-level opacity as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",

  "old_string": "DIF should be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal; \\Cref{sec:downstream}), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles.\n\nOur contributions span empirical findings and methodological adaptations for the LLM evaluation setting:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Two dimensions of benchmark behavior}: We empirically quantify a theoretically expected but previously unverified distinction: global exposure (web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}) and differential functioning (DIF flags) are empirically independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$ on 9,229 items)---complementary diagnostic signals that capture different aspects of how benchmarks change over time (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: We establish that ${\\approx}$27--29\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF after non-independence corrections; replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Three methodological adaptations for LLM evaluation}: We develop and validate an audit framework addressing three challenges specific to LLM benchmarks: (a)~a novel cross-subject matching strategy solves matching-variable contamination when the benchmark itself may be compromised, with a constructive $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee ($k \\leq 5$; \\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}); (b)~family-level DEFF correction, adapted from survey methodology, handles non-independence among model families; (c)~semi-synthetic validation provides calibrated detection power absent ground-truth DIF labels. Replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee}: A cross-subject matching strategy provides a constructive $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee under single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$), solving matching-variable corruption when the benchmark itself may be compromised (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).     "newString": "Our contributions are:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale, orthogonal to contamination labels}: We establish that ${\\approx}$27--29\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts---a signal empirically independent of web-overlap contamination labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$)---revealing that global exposure and differential functioning capture complementary aspects of benchmark change. Replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee}: A cross-subject matching strategy provides a constructive $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee under single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$), solving matching-variable corruption when the benchmark itself may be compromised (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We complement this with family-level DEFF correction for non-independence and semi-synthetic validation for detection-power calibration.\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected across time-defined cohorts---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} we find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.18\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. The incomparability is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are empirically independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Schematic illustration of global vs.\\ differential contamination via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training (assuming no ceiling effect). The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (${\\approx}$27--29\\% after non-independence corrections; conservative bound: 13.5\\%) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}).\n\nDIF should be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal; \\Cref{sec:downstream}), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles.\n\nOur contributions span empirical findings and methodological adaptations for the LLM evaluation setting:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Two dimensions of benchmark behavior}: We empirically quantify a theoretically expected but previously unverified distinction: global exposure (web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}) and differential functioning (DIF flags) are empirically independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$ on 9,229 items)---complementary diagnostic signals that capture different aspects of how benchmarks change over time (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: We establish that ${\\approx}$27--29\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF after non-independence corrections; replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Three methodological adaptations for LLM evaluation}: We develop and validate an audit framework addressing three challenges specific to LLM benchmarks: (a)~a novel cross-subject matching strategy solves matching-variable contamination when the benchmark itself may be compromised, with a constructive $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee ($k \\leq 5$; \\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}); (b)~family-level DEFF correction, adapted from survey methodology, handles non-independence among model families; (c)~semi-synthetic validation provides calibrated detection power absent ground-truth DIF labels. Replicated on GSM8K (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

  "old_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\enlargethispage*{4\\baselineskip}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design. Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe two temporal cohorts differ in base/instruct composition (2023: 79.7\\% vs.\\ 2024: 67.9\\% base).     "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "We do not modify the core MH statistic; our adaptations address challenges specific to the LLM evaluation setting (cross-subject matching for contamination control, DEFF correction for non-independence, semi-synthetic validation absent ground-truth labels).",
    "newString": "We retain the standard MH statistic and develop LLM-specific adaptations around it: cross-subject matching for contamination control (with a formal $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee), DEFF correction for non-independence, and semi-synthetic validation absent ground-truth labels.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\\% \\citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \\citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues and \\citet{oren2024proving} prove contamination through exchangeability tests. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique,deng2024investigating} or in-context learning effects~\\citep{zawalski2026codec}. \\citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \\citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \\citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, IRT-based evaluation quality~\\citep{kraus2024irt}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses IRT calibration to establish anchor items for cross-temporal score equating via fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. While Growing Pains prescribes how to equate scores after instability is detected, our work diagnoses \\emph{which} items exhibit instability and quantifies its magnitude---the diagnostic step that must precede equating. Their anchor-item selection can directly consume our stable-item set as pre-screened candidates; our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF identifies which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability---contamination detection and psychometric evaluation optimization have developed as separate threads.\n\n\\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{millsap1993dif}, \\citet{zumbo1999handbook}, and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive reviews. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the core MH statistic; our adaptations address challenges specific to the LLM evaluation setting (cross-subject matching for contamination control, DEFF correction for non-independence, semi-synthetic validation absent ground-truth labels). The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\nSeveral concurrent approaches address benchmark reliability from complementary angles. The Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} perform meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items, and MMLU-Pro~\\citep{wang2024mmlupro} extends item difficulty; DIF monitoring provides a lower-cost alternative when reconstruction is infeasible, identifying which existing items remain measurement-invariant rather than replacing them. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} use LLMs to predict DIF in human assessments---the reverse direction; we apply DIF to LLM benchmarks with models as examinees. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} and self-evolving update frameworks~\\citep{ying2024automating,xu2025benchmark} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Leaderboard rankings are sensitive to minor evaluation perturbations~\\citep{alzahrani2024benchmarks}. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and item-level opacity as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",

Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, IRT-based evaluation quality~\\citep{kraus2024irt}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. We do not modify the core MH statistic; our adaptations address challenges specific to the LLM evaluation setting (cross-subject matching for contamination control, DEFF correction for non-independence, semi-synthetic validation absent ground-truth labels). The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).",
          "+The Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{millsap1993dif}, \\citet{zumbo1999handbook}, and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive reviews. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We retain the standard MH statistic and develop LLM-specific adaptations around it: cross-subject matching for contamination control (with a formal $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee), DEFF correction for non-independence, and semi-synthetic validation absent ground-truth labels. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}",
          " Several concurrent approaches address benchmark reliability from complementary angles. The Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} perform meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items, and MMLU-Pro~\\citep{wang2024mmlupro} extends item difficulty; DIF monitoring provides a lower-cost alternative when reconstruction is infeasible, identifying which existing items remain measurement-invariant rather than replacing them. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} use LLMs to predict DIF in human assessments---the reverse direction; we apply DIF to LLM benchmarks with models as examinees. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} and self-evolving update frameworks~\\citep{ying2024automating,xu2025benchmark} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Leaderboard rankings are sensitive to minor evaluation perturbations~\\citep{alzahrani2024benchmarks}. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and item-level opacity as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },

    "newString": "\\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected across time-defined cohorts---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} we find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.18\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. The incomparability is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are empirically independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Schematic illustration of global vs.\\ differential contamination via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training (assuming no ceiling effect). The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (${\\approx}$27--29\\% after non-independence corrections; conservative bound: 13.5\\%) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale, orthogonal to contamination labels}: We establish that ${\\approx}$27--29\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts---a signal empirically independent of web-overlap contamination labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$)---revealing that global exposure and differential functioning capture complementary aspects of benchmark change. Replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee}: A cross-subject matching strategy provides a constructive $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee under single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$), solving matching-variable corruption when the benchmark itself may be compromised (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We complement this with family-level DEFF correction for non-independence and semi-synthetic validation for detection-power calibration.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} we find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.18\\% under a random-split control.           " \\end{equation}",
          " ",
          "-The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. These thresholds were calibrated for human test-taker populations; we retain them as standardized reference points and verify robustness across stricter cutoffs (\\Cref{sec:temporal}). Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. At $N = 5{,}227$, significance ($p < 0.05$) is nearly automatically satisfied for any $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$: only 3 of 3{,}918 C items lack significance, so the dual criterion effectively reduces to a single effect-size threshold; per-item DEFF correction (\\Cref{app:deff}) restores $p$-value selectivity by reducing effective $N$: under simple-mean DEFF, 269 items (6.9\\%) lose significance while retaining $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 74\\% of these are near-miss ($p_{\\mathrm{adj}} \\in [0.05, 0.10]$).",
          "+The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. These thresholds were calibrated for human test-taker populations; we retain them as standardized reference points and verify robustness across stricter cutoffs (\\Cref{sec:temporal}). Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. At $N = 1{,}887$ (the temporal comparison; 5{,}227 total across all cohort designs), significance ($p < 0.05$) is nearly automatically satisfied for any $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$: only 3 of 3{,}918 C items lack significance, so the dual criterion effectively reduces to a single effect-size threshold; per-item DEFF correction (\\Cref{app:deff}) restores $p$-value selectivity by reducing effective $N$: under simple-mean DEFF, 269 items (6.9\\%) lose significance while retaining $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 74\\% of these are near-miss ($p_{\\mathrm{adj}} \\in [0.05, 0.10]$).",
          " ",
          " The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\\Cref{sec:structural}).",
          " "

Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond MCQ-format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power, and detection power under realistic contamination patterns (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) is untested. The framework detects \\emph{that} items function differently but not \\emph{why}---a deliberate design choice that limits immediate actionability; follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Three convergent corrections (${\\approx}$27--29\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\\% CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\\citep{french2010hierarchical}. (3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.069 when contamination spans ${\\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp; \\Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to open-ended or partial-credit benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric.\n",

   **B. 补充数据: Base-only / Instruct-only Temporal DIF (completed)**: Add table with base-only (C%=32.1%) and instruct-only (C%=36.5%) temporal DIF data to appendix, with §5.1 reference.
   
   **C. R16 审稿综合修改 (JUST COMPLETED, needs visual verification)**: Major revision with hard constraint of strict ≤8 pages main text, 0 \enlargethispage. Includes contribution restructure (3→2), Abstract/Discussion/RelatedWork compression, and multiple WARNING/INFO fixes. DDL 5/25.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - MH-DIF with ETS severity classification (A/B/C categories)
   - DEFF (Design Effect) correction: simple-mean (29.2%) as recommended primary estimate vs weighted-mean (13.5%) as conservative lower bound
   - Temporal score incomparability (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts)
   - Cross-subject matching with formal ΔFPR=0 guarantee (k≤5)
   - Semi-synthetic injection: "stylized" uniform injection (not "worst-case"); non-uniform ΔTPR≈0.41
   - Base-only (C%=32.1%) vs instruct-only (C%=36.5%) temporal DIF
   - Impact-bias framework (Camilli & Shepard 1994)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text strict limit, Limitations/refs/appendix unlimited, 0 \enlargethispage target

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **introduction.tex** (MODIFIED - R16 N2 + M1)
     - Contribution restructure from 3→2; bridge paragraph removed; terminology parenthetical added
     ```latex
     % M1 parenthetical:
     \emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected
     
     % N2 - New 2-contribution structure:
     Our contributions are:
     \begin{enumerate}
     \item \textbf{Temporal DIF at scale, orthogonal to contamination labels}: We establish that ${\approx}$27--29\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts---a signal empirically independent of web-overlap contamination labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$)---revealing that global exposure and differential functioning capture complementary aspects of benchmark change. Replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).
     \item \textbf{Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee}: A cross-subject matching strategy provides a constructive $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee under single-subject contamination ($k \leq 5$), solving matching-variable corruption when the benchmark itself may be compromised (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We complement this with family-level DEFF correction for non-independence and semi-synthetic validation for detection-power calibration.
     \end{enumerate}
     ```

     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     LLM benchmark scores are compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models), validated through a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (${\approx}$27--29\% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; conservative bound 13.5\%; random-split baseline 0.18\%). Web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of DIF flags: global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Temporal DIF does not threaten aggregate rankings ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but reveals structured item-level change---base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF patterns---and shifts DIF-corrected scores by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power, and detection power under realistic contamination patterns (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) is untested. The framework detects \\emph{that} items function differently but not \\emph{why}---a deliberate design choice that limits immediate actionability; follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Three convergent corrections (${\\approx}$27--29\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\\% CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\\citep{french2010hierarchical}. (3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.069 when contamination spans ${\\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp; \\Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to open-ended or partial-credit benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric. Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond MCQ-format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.\n",

        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh,magis2010difr} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. These thresholds were calibrated for human test-taker populations; we retain them as standardized reference points and verify robustness across stricter cutoffs (\\Cref{sec:temporal}). Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. At $N = 1{,}887$ (the temporal comparison; 5{,}227 total across all cohort designs), significance ($p < 0.05$) is nearly automatically satisfied for any $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$: only 3 of 3{,}918 C items lack significance, so the dual criterion effectively reduces to a single effect-size threshold; per-item DEFF correction (\\Cref{app:deff}) restores $p$-value selectivity by reducing effective $N$: under simple-mean DEFF, 269 items (6.9\\%) lose significance while retaining $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 74\\% of these are near-miss ($p_{\\mathrm{adj}} \\in [0.05, 0.10]$).\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\\Cref{sec:structural}).\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces four challenges absent in educational testing. First, overlapping training corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1{,}000 replications shuffling group labels within score strata) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$~pp under cross-subject matching (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, bimodal ability distributions motivate quintile-based stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57-subject structure raises a multidimensionality concern~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf,roussos1996multidimensionality}: domain-level ability profiles are nearly flat across cohorts (Cohen's $d$ range $= 0.12$), cross-subject and within-subject matching agree ($\\kappa = 0.80$, 834 items), and cross-subject-unique C items do not concentrate in high-shift subjects (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nWe construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window; (3)~\\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a stylized uniform injection pattern, reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power; realistic contamination (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) would produce weaker signals.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$). $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ is a constructive guarantee of the matching strategy, independent of injection realism, whereas $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ may overestimate detection power under the stylized uniform injection. $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ remains negligible for single-domain contamination ($k \\leq 5$); when contamination spans many domains, within-subject matching should be preferred (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol.\n\nFollowing DIF convention~\\citep{wainer1993differential}, we do not apply family-wise error correction: the dual ETS~C threshold ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ \\emph{and} $p < 0.05$) guards against false positives; BH-FDR at $q = 0.05$ reclassifies only 3 of 3{,}918 C items (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Diagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3, domain-specificity, DIF decomposition) and further details appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",
        "numLines": 59,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 59
      }
    },

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} we find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.18\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. The incomparability is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are empirically independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Schematic illustration of global vs.\\ differential contamination via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training (assuming no ceiling effect). The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (${\\approx}$27--29\\% after non-independence corrections; conservative bound: 13.5\\%) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale, orthogonal to contamination labels}: We establish that ${\\approx}$27--29\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts---a signal empirically independent of web-overlap contamination labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$)---revealing that global exposure and differential functioning capture complementary aspects of benchmark change. Replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee}: A cross-subject matching strategy provides a constructive $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee under single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$), solving matching-variable corruption when the benchmark itself may be compromised (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We complement this with family-level DEFF correction for non-independence and semi-synthetic validation for detection-power calibration.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 24,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 24
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/introduction.tex"
  },

  "timestamp": "2026-05-23T10:55:11.107Z",
