      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nA Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF: 31\\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[29.4\\%, 38.1\\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$, upper bound driven by mega-family over-representation; \\Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect in parallel: simple-mean DEFF (29.2\\%) and cluster bootstrap (26.7\\%) converge on ${\\approx}$27--29\\%, while the conservative weighted-mean DEFF yields 13.5\\%---still $75\\times$ the random baseline (\\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation}). We recommend simple-mean DEFF as the primary estimate: it converges with the cluster bootstrap and DEFF simulation, while weighted-mean DEFF over-corrects by down-weighting large families with systematically lower ICC. No single family drives the signal (LOFO $|\\Delta\\mathrm{C\\%}| \\leq 2.93$\\,pp); graduated de-duplication confirms C\\% scales with power, not redundancy (\\Cref{app:dedup_k}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield C\\% of 0.18\\% (2023) and 1.3\\% (2024), both ${\\geq}23\\times$ below temporal C\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$, confirming at most ${\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). Domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); a Simpson's paradox confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate gains (OR reverses from 1.55 to 0.73 after controlling for difficulty; \\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. C\\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \\citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\\ labels are not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & C\\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,600 / ${\\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2024 (sanity) & ${\\sim}$400 / ${\\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals---an empirical verification of the theoretically expected but previously untested distinction between global exposure and differential functioning (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[0.94, 1.02]$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests confirm near-independence ($\\phi = 0.012$, OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15], Jaccard $= 0.206$, within permutation null $[0.203, 0.222]$; $p = 0.88$). This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes. The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The two constructs---global exposure and differential functioning---are empirically orthogonal.\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$; cross-subject C\\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\\% converges to ${\\sim}$28--29\\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Structural controls and sensitivity checks for temporal DIF. None reduces C\\% below 31\\%; $\\theta$-conditioning serves as a sensitivity check confirming total-score adequacy.}\n\\label{tab:structural_comparison}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nApproach & Mechanism & Temp.\\ C\\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\\ total score & $0.314_{\\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\\pm.027}$ & Reference \\\\\nMatched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\\ DIF & ${\\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\\ 0.94--1.04 \\\\\n$\\theta$-cond.\\ (sens.) & $\\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\\theta,\\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\\\\nWithin-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\\% $= 38.1\\%$ ($>$25\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with detection-power upper bounds of $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; stylized uniform injection; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination spanning $k = 10$ subjects (18\\% of domains), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.047 \\pm 0.022$; at $k = 20$, to $0.069 \\pm 0.018$ (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection,app:fig_matching}). Even worst-case multi-subject inflation ($\\sim$7~pp) cannot account for the observed 31\\% C\\% rate, which exceeds the 0.18\\% random baseline by ${>}170\\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, worst-case $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ (0.069) represents up to 50\\% of the 13.5\\% lower bound, underscoring $k \\leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (non-uniform flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), with distinguishable $\\rho$ signatures (capability-like: $+0.77$ vs.\\ contamination-like: $-0.52$; zero seed overlap), though mapping to real-data mechanisms requires further work.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nStable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks ($\\rho = 0.997$, $\\tau = 0.958$; flip rate $\\leq 2.8\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Among the 200 most displaced models (top 3.8\\%), 33.6\\% of pairwise rankings reverse, concentrating among closely-ranked pairs (35.2\\% at $\\Delta\\mathrm{rank} < 50$ vs.\\ 0.0\\% above 1000; \\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ random; $p < 0.001$). DIF-corrected scoring (stable items only) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with a cohort-differential of $0.29$\\,pp ($d = 0.31$; individual rank displacements reach ${\\pm}800$; \\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ vs.\\ $17.9$--$18.9\\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\\% = ETS~C items ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts; $\\rho$/$\\tau$ = Spearman/Kendall correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip = pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio = C\\%/Rand.\\ C\\%, with baseline varying by benchmark. All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.18 & 174$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K is consistent with detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory cross-benchmark signal, GSM1K contamination gaps correlate with DIF-C item accuracy ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}), but the small sample requires replication.\n",
        "numLines": 119,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 119
      }

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---and this is by design. Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($\\rho = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\rho = -0.079$; $\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). Following the impact-bias framework~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, this reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nComposition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp), base-only (32.1\\%), and instruct-only (36.5\\%) temporal analyses independently confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}). These findings have immediate implications for leaderboard maintenance: temporal DIF means the same score carries different meaning across model generations, motivating periodic psychometric audits alongside score reporting.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~the observed C\\% (${\\approx}$27--29\\%) substantially exceeds typical educational DIF rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$), unlike educational settings where DIF correlates with construct-irrelevant variance; (3)~model families introduce non-independence (design effect) absent in human testing, necessitating DEFF correction (\\Cref{app:deff}).\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend MH-DIF audits whenever ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate ($K{=}3$ for smaller corpora; \\Cref{tab:k3_power}), with action thresholds calibrated to $\\rho$-degradation (\\Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}). Flagged items warrant mechanism-conditional responses: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal; DIF on web-clean items is inconsistent with contamination as sole mechanism (annotate; DIF alone cannot determine causation); base/instruct direction reversal motivates refined cohort definitions. DIF-corrected scoring motivates equating adjustments~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}.\n",
        "numLines": 21,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 21
      }
    },

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	\looseness=-2
5	Across six METABENCH benchmarks, 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts (${\approx}$27--29\% for MMLU; conservative bound: 13.5\%), while aggregate rankings remain preserved ($\rho \approx 0.997$). Global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions (Jaccard $= 0.206$), confirmed by opposite base-vs-instruct DIF patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$). DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average, with rank displacements exceeding ${\pm}500$. Mechanism-conditional DIF audit should complement contamination screening as standard practice~\citep{habba2026growingpains}.
6	
```

_source line 5849 · 2026-05-23T10:56:52.151Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Fg2DZwntKPJwABiieKD2av`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models), validated through a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (${\approx}$27--29\% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; conservative bound 13.5\%; random-split baseline 0.18\%). Web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of DIF flags: global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Temporal DIF does not threaten aggregate rankings ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but reveals structured item-level change---base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF patterns---and shifts DIF-corrected scores by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

```text
155	\section{Design Effect Estimation}
156	\label{app:deff}
157	
158	Model families (e.g., Mistral, Llama-3) induce intra-class correlation (ICC) that inflates Mantel-Haenszel $\chi^2$ statistics beyond the nominal $\chi^2_1$ reference distribution. We estimate the design effect (DEFF) per item following \citet{rao1992variance}: $\mathrm{DEFF}_i = 1 + (\bar{m} - 1) \cdot \mathrm{ICC}_i$, where $\bar{m}$ is the mean family size and $\mathrm{ICC}_i$ is the one-way random-effects ICC for item $i$ across 30 families (restricted to ref+foc cohort; 1,884 models with family$\geq$2).
159	
160	Two estimates of $\bar{m}$ bracket the adjustment: (1)~\emph{Simple mean} ($\bar{m} = 62.8$), treating all families equally, yields $\mathrm{DEFF} = 3.87$ and adjusted C\% $= 29.2\%$; (2)~\emph{Weighted mean} ($\bar{m} = \sum n_f^2 / \sum n_f = 254.5$; \citealp{kish1965survey}), reflecting the effective cluster size experienced by a randomly sampled model, yields $\mathrm{DEFF} = 12.79$ and adjusted C\% $= 13.5\%$.
161	
162	\noindent Mean ICC $= 0.047$ (median $0.041$, Q3 $= 0.061$, max $= 0.219$; 4.8\% of items exceed $0.10$). After dividing each item's $\chi^2_{\mathrm{MH}}$ by its item-specific DEFF and re-applying ETS thresholds, 13.5--29.2\% of items retain Category~C classification depending on the $\bar{m}$ variant---both far exceeding the 0.18\% random-split baseline (75--162$\times$).
163	
164	\paragraph{Size-stratified DEFF.} The aggregate ICC masks a size--ICC inverse relationship: smaller families exhibit higher within-family similarity (\Cref{tab:deff_stratified}). Tier-level ICC ranges from 0.246 (2--10 members) to 0.082 ({>}200 members), reflecting greater heterogeneity in mega-families. At small family sizes, DEFF correction has negligible impact (Adj.\ C\% $= 31.3\%$); the conservative 13.5\% bound is driven entirely by the two mega-families (Mistral, Llama-3). Family definition sensitivity is minimal: merging sub-generations (e.g., Llama-2 + Llama-3) into coarse families (22 families) changes C\% by $\leq$0.3~pp.
165	
166	\begin{table}[h]
167	\centering
168	\caption{Size-stratified DEFF analysis. ICC and DEFF computed per tier; Adj.\ C\% applies tier-specific DEFF to all items.}
169	\label{tab:deff_stratified}
170	\small
171	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrc}
172	\toprule
173	Tier & Families & $\bar{m}$ & ICC & DEFF & Adj.\ C\% \\
174	\midrule
```

354	Three of four lineages show C\% above the random baseline (0.18--1.3\%). Llama is the best-powered comparison ($N = 486$ total) and yields raw C\% $= 59.2\%$, which combines temporal and generational effects. After ability matching, within-architecture C\% converges to ${\sim}$28--29\%, consistent with temporal C\% ($28.9\%$)---confirming that the temporal DIF signal persists within individual lineages and is not an artifact of comparing different architectures.
355	
356	\section{Base-Only and Instruct-Only Temporal DIF}
357	\label{app:baseinst_temporal}
358	
359	Separating the temporal cohorts by model type confirms that both base and instruct models independently exhibit substantial temporal DIF (\Cref{tab:baseinst_temporal}). Training methodology modulates DIF \emph{direction} (base: 60.1\% reference-favoring with $\rho = -0.570$; instruct: 65.6\% focal-favoring with $\rho = -0.079$) rather than DIF \emph{magnitude}, providing direct evidence against composition shift as the primary driver.

200	DEFF (weighted $\bar{m}$) & Size-proportional weighting & Adjusted C\% = 13.5\% \\
201	Graduated de-dup.\ ($K{=}20$) & Remove within-family duplicates & C\% = 19.7\% $\pm$ 0.9\% \\
202	\bottomrule
203	\end{tabular}}
204	\label{tab:uncertainty}
205	\end{table}
206	
207	\paragraph{Half-year granularity.} Splitting at the 6-month boundary yields C\% $= 34.4\%$, slightly above the annual 31.3\%. The higher rate likely reflects more polarized cohort composition: fewer models span both half-year periods, producing reference and focal groups with less overlapping ability distributions and consequently inflated DIF detection rates.
208	
209	The cluster bootstrap upper bound (53.2\%) is driven by extreme bootstrap resamples that over-represent Mistral ($n = 547$) or Llama-3 ($n = 315$), producing atypical cohort compositions. Two independent methods---cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\%) and simple-mean DEFF adjustment (29.2\%)---converge on $\sim$27--29\%, establishing a robust corrected estimate that remains $>$150$\times$ the random-split baseline (0.18\%). Graduated de-duplication at $K{=}20$ yields C\% $= 19.7\% \pm 0.9\%$ (random member selection, 5 draws), lower than the corrected estimate due to reduced statistical power ($N \approx 450$ vs.\ 1,887), but within-$K$ variance ${<}1.5$\,pp at all $K$ confirms the signal is power-driven rather than redundancy-inflated (\Cref{app:dedup_k}). The weighted-mean DEFF (13.5\%) represents a conservative extreme; even this lower bound exceeds the baseline by 75$\times$.
210	
211	\section{Cross-Benchmark Comparison}
212	\label{app:gsm8k_details}
213	
214	\begin{table}[h]
215	\centering
216	\caption{Cross-benchmark comparison of DIF analysis on MMLU and GSM8K. Both benchmarks exhibit temporal score incomparability with validated semi-synthetic detection power. Standard and external matching use different exploitation rates ($p$) because each benchmark's optimal operating point differs; external matching results are compared at $p = 0.5$ for both. Subscript $\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}
217	\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}
218	\small
219	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
220	\toprule
221	Metric & MMLU & GSM8K \\
222	\midrule
223	Items & 12,508 & 1,319 \\
224	Models & 5,227 & 6,702 \\
225	Temporal C\% & 31.3\% & 18.9\% \\
226	Sanity C\% & 0.18\% & 0.3\% \\
227	$\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\pm.006}$ \\
228	$\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\pm.028}$ \\
229	\bottomrule
230	\end{tabular}
231	\end{table}

Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}

Subscript $\\pm$ values denote standard deviations across 5 random injection seeds.}\n\\label{tab:gsm8k_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMetric & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nItems & 12,508 & 1,319 \\\\\nModels & 5,227 & 6,702 \\\\\nTemporal C\\% & 31.3\\% & 18.9\\% \\\\\nSanity C\\% & 0.18\\% & 0.3\\% \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.7$, standard) & -- & $+0.818_{\\pm.006}$ \\\\\n$\\Delta$TPR ($p{=}0.5$, external) & $+0.508_{\\pm.027}$ & $+0.514_{\\pm.028}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{app:figures}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). ```text
80	\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}
81	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
82	
83	\looseness=-1
84	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with detection-power upper bounds of $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; stylized uniform injection; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \leq 5$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination spanning $k = 10$ subjects (18\% of domains), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.047 \pm 0.022$; at $k = 20$, to $0.069 \pm 0.018$ (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection,app:fig_matching}). Even worst-case multi-subject inflation ($\sim$7~pp) cannot account for the observed 31\% C\% rate, which exceeds the 0.18\% random baseline by ${>}170\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, worst-case $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ (0.069) represents up to 50\% of the 13.5\% lower bound, underscoring $k \leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (non-uniform flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\ $0.51$ uniform), with distinguishable $\rho$ signatures (capability-like: $+0.77$ vs.\ contamination-like: $-0.52$; zero seed overlap), though mapping to real-data mechanisms requires further work.
85	
86	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
87	\label{sec:downstream}
88	
89	\looseness=-1

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MdxAnrLuULhG6Vg7WYNc78`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} we find that 31\% of MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.18\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\rho \approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. The incomparability is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.
5	
```

    "newString": "versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} we find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.18\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. The incomparability is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are empirically independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Schematic illustration of global vs.\\ differential contamination via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training (assuming no ceiling effect). The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (${\\approx}$27--29\\% after non-independence corrections; conservative bound: 13.5\\%) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale, orthogonal to contamination labels}: We establish that ${\\approx}$27--29\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts---a signal empirically independent of web-overlap contamination labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$)---revealing that global exposure and differential functioning capture complementary aspects of benchmark change. Replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee}: A cross-subject matching strategy provides a constructive $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee under single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$), solving matching-variable corruption when the benchmark itself may be compromised (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We complement this with family-level DEFF correction for non-independence and semi-synthetic validation for detection-power calibration.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

          " \\label{sec:semisynthetic}",
          " ",
          " \\looseness=-1",
          "-Cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with detection-power upper bounds of $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; stylized uniform injection; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination spanning $k = 10$ subjects (18\\% of domains), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.047 \\pm 0.022$; at $k = 20$, to $0.069 \\pm 0.018$ (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection,app:fig_matching}). Even worst-case multi-subject inflation ($\\sim$7~pp) cannot account for the observed 31\\% C\\% rate, which exceeds the 0.18\\% random baseline by ${>}170\\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, worst-case $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ (0.069) represents up to 50\\% of the 13.5\\% lower bound, underscoring $k \\leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (non-uniform flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), with distinguishable $\\rho$ signatures (capability-like: $+0.77$ vs.\\ contamination-like: $-0.52$; zero seed overlap), though mapping to real-data mechanisms requires further work.",
          "+Cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with detection-power upper bounds of $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; stylized uniform injection; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01U54xUQThHVjNBDmzdGoQKe`</summary>

```text
12	\subsection{Temporal DIF: 31\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}
13	\label{sec:temporal}
14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\% CI: $[29.4\%, 38.1\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$, upper bound driven by mega-family over-representation; \Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect in parallel: simple-mean DEFF (29.2\%) and cluster bootstrap (26.7\%) converge on ${\approx}$27--29\%, while the conservative weighted-mean DEFF yields 13.5\%---still $75\times$ the random baseline (\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation}). We recommend simple-mean DEFF as the primary estimate: it converges with the cluster bootstrap and DEFF simulation, while weighted-mean DEFF over-corrects by down-weighting large families with systematically lower ICC. No single family drives the signal (LOFO $|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp); graduated de-duplication confirms C\% scales with power, not redundancy (\Cref{app:dedup_k}).
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\% of 0.62\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\% (2024; 100 permutations), both ${\geq}24\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
20	
21	\looseness=-1
22	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). Domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); a Simpson's paradox confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate gains (OR reverses from 1.55 to 0.73 after controlling for difficulty; \Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
23	
```

The higher rate likely reflects more polarized cohort composition: fewer models span both half-year periods, producing reference and focal groups with less overlapping ability distributions and consequently inflated DIF detection rates.",
          " ",
          "-The cluster bootstrap upper bound (53.2\\%) is driven by extreme bootstrap resamples that over-represent Mistral ($n = 547$) or Llama-3 ($n = 315$), producing atypical cohort compositions. Two independent methods---cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\\%) and simple-mean DEFF adjustment (29.2\\%)---converge on $\\sim$27--29\\%, establishing a robust corrected estimate that remains $>$45$\\times$ the random-split baseline (0.62\\%). Graduated de-duplication at $K{=}20$ yields C\\% $= 19.7\\% \\pm 0.9\\%$ (random member selection, 5 draws), lower than the corrected estimate due to reduced statistical power ($N \\approx 450$ vs.\\ 1,887), but within-$K$ variance ${<}1.5$\\,pp at all $K$ confirms the signal is power-driven rather than redundancy-inflated (\\Cref{app:dedup_k}). The weighted-mean DEFF (13.5\\%) represents a conservative extreme; even this lower bound exceeds the baseline by 75$\\times$.",

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] R17 审稿综合修改。DDL 5/25，严格 8 页。所有修改必须 net-neutral 或减少字数。\n\n---\n\n## CRITICAL × 2\n\n### N1: DEFF 区间表述 + 打破循环论证\n\n**核心修改**: 删除 \"We recommend simple-mean DEFF as the primary estimate\" 那句话。改为区间表述。\n\n§4.1 (experiments.tex L16) 修改 DEFF 段落为：\n- \"Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2% and weighted-mean DEFF yields 13.5%, bracketing temporal C% at 13.5–29.2%. The cluster bootstrap 95% CI ([26.7%, 53.2%])—a family-level resampling method independent of DEFF methodology—excludes the weighted-mean estimate (13.5%), providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound. Under weighted-mean DEFF (C% = 13.5%), all downstream conclusions hold: ρ = 0.999, score shift = +1.77 pp (Appendix D).\"\n- 删除原来关于 \"simple-mean converges with cluster bootstrap and DEFF simulation, while weighted-mean over-corrects by down-weighting large families with systematically lower ICC\" 的句子（这是被指循环的论证）。\n\nAbstract: \"≈27–29% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; conservative bound 13.5%\" → \"13.5–29.2% for MMLU after non-independence corrections\"\n\nIntroduction (L4): 同步修改，使用区间表述 \"13.5–29.2% for MMLU after non-independence corrections\"\n\nConclusion: 同步 \"≈27–29%\" → \"13.5–29.2%\"\n\n**注意 Reviewer 指出的数量混淆**: 17.9–31.3%（跨 6 benchmark 原始 C%）和 13.5–29.2%（MMLU DEFF 修正）是不同度量。Abstract 必须保留两个层次。格式参考：\"17.9–31.3% of items exhibit large temporal DIF across six benchmarks (13.5–29.2% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; ≥22× random baseline)\"\n\n### N2: ΔTPR 术语修正 + difficulty-dependent 进主文\n\n全文搜索替换：\n- \"detection-power upper bound\" → \"reference-case detection power\" \n- \"detection-power upper bounds\" → \"reference-case detection power\"\n- 首次出现时加括号定义 \"(under stylized uniform injection; §3.4)\"\n\n§4.5 (experiments.tex L84) 加入 difficulty-dependent 结果并列：\n在现有 difficulty-dependent injection 句子前加上下文：\"Among tested injection variants, uniform injection produces the strongest signal; difficulty-dependent injection (flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields ΔTPR ≈ 0.41 at p = 0.5 (vs. 0.51 uniform), a 20% reduction reflecting contamination heterogeneity.\"\n\nLimitations: 加 1 句 partial memorization caveat：\"Partial memorization scenarios—where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping—would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model.\"\n\n§4.1 power analysis (L10): TPR 0.68–0.94 加限定 \"(under uniform injection)\"\n\n---\n\n## WARNING × 6\n\n### W1: Mechanism-agnostic framing\n§3.4 (method.tex) injection framework 段落开头加 1 句：\n\"We use contamination injection as a controlled case study to validate DIF detection power; DIF itself is mechanism-agnostic, diagnosing that items function differently without attributing cause (§5.2).\"\n\n### W2: Growing Pains 方法论对比\n§2 (related_work.tex) Growing Pains 段落，替换现有 \"While Growing Pains prescribes...\" 句子为 2 句：\n\"While Growing Pains uses parametric IRT (2PL/3PL fixed-parameter calibration), MH-DIF is nonparametric and robust to model misspecification—an advantage given poor 2PL fit for MMLU (RMSEA > 0.08; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}). Growing Pains prescribes how to equate scores after instability is detected; our work diagnoses which items exhibit instability and quantifies severity via ETS classification, providing pre-screened anchor candidates for downstream equating.\"\n\n### W3: ΔFPR k 限定\nAbstract: \"cross-subject matching\" 附近，如有 ΔFPR=0，加条件 \"(k ≤ 5 subjects)\"\nIntroduction: 同步检查 ΔFPR=0 是否有 k 条件\n§4.5: 现有 k 数据已充足（k=10: 0.047, k=20: 0.069）。加 1 句经验约束：\n\"Web-overlap annotations suggest that the majority of MMLU subjects have contamination prevalence below 50%, empirically constraining k; non-web contamination channels could affect additional domains, though the k ≤ 5 guarantee covers the documented contamination regime.\"\n\n### W4: 正交性替代解释\n§5.1 末尾（替换教育 DIF 对比的第 3 点以控制字数，因为该点价值最低），加：\n\"The near-independence admits two interpretations: (1) DIF captures a broader signal—including capability evolution and training methodology shifts—of which contamination is one component; (2) DIF and contamination detection measure distinct constructs. ## CRITICAL × 2

### N1: DEFF 区间表述 + 打破循环论证

**核心修改**: 删除 "We recommend simple-mean DEFF as the primary estimate" 那句话。改为区间表述。

§4.1 (experiments.tex L16) 修改 DEFF 段落为：
- "Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2% and weighted-mean DEFF yields 13.5%, bracketing temporal C% at 13.5–29.2%. The cluster bootstrap 95% CI ([26.7%, 53.2%])—a family-level resampling method independent of DEFF methodology—excludes the weighted-mean estimate (13.5%), providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound. Under weighted-mean DEFF (C% = 13.5%), all downstream conclusions hold: ρ = 0.999, score shift = +1.77 pp (Appendix D)."
- 删除原来关于 "simple-mean converges with cluster bootstrap and DEFF simulation, while weighted-mean over-corrects by down-weighting large families with systematically lower ICC" 的句子（这是被指循环的论证）。

Abstract: "≈27–29% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; conservative bound 13.5%" → "13.5–29.2% for MMLU after non-independence corrections"

Introduction (L4): 同步修改，使用区间表述 "13.5–29.2% for MMLU after non-independence corrections"

Conclusion: 同步 "≈27–29%" → "13.5–29.2%"

**注意 Reviewer 指出的数量混淆**: 17.9–31.3%（跨 6 benchmark 原始 C%）和 13.5–29.2%（MMLU DEFF 修正）是不同度量。Abstract 必须保留两个层次。格式参考："17.9–31.3% of items exhibit large temporal DIF across six benchmarks (13.5–29.2% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; ≥22× random baseline)"

### N2: ΔTPR 术语修正 + difficulty-dependent 进主文

全文搜索替换：
- "detection-power upper bound" → "reference-case detection power" 
- "detection-power upper bounds" → "reference-case detection power"
- 首次出现时加括号定义 "(under stylized uniform injection; §3.4)"

§4.5 (experiments.tex L84) 加入 difficulty-dependent 结果并列：
在现有 difficulty-dependent injection 句子前加上下文："Among tested injection variants, uniform injection produces the strongest signal; difficulty-dependent injection (flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields ΔTPR ≈ 0.41 at p = 0.5 (vs. 0.51 uniform), a 20% reduction reflecting contamination heterogeneity."

Limitations: 加 1 句 partial memorization caveat："Partial memorization scenarios—where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping—would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model."

§4.1 power analysis (L10): TPR 0.68–0.94 加限定 "(under uniform injection)"

---

## WARNING × 6

### W1: Mechanism-agnostic framing
§3.4 (method.tex) injection framework 段落开头加 1 句：
"We use contamination injection as a controlled case study to validate DIF detection power; DIF itself is mechanism-agnostic, diagnosing that items function differently without attributing cause (§5.2)."

### W2: Growing Pains 方法论对比
§2 (related_work.tex) Growing Pains 段落，替换现有 "While Growing Pains prescribes..." 句子为 2 句：
"While Growing Pains uses parametric IRT (2PL/3PL fixed-parameter calibration), MH-DIF is nonparametric and robust to model misspecification—an advantage given poor 2PL fit for MMLU (RMSEA > 0.08; \Cref{app:power_sensitivity}). Growing Pains prescribes how to equate scores after instability is detected; our work diagnoses which items exhibit instability and quantifies severity via ETS classification, providing pre-screened anchor candidates for downstream equating."

### W3: ΔFPR k 限定
Abstract: "cross-subject matching" 附近，如有 ΔFPR=0，加条件 "(k ≤ 5 subjects)"
Introduction: 同步检查 ΔFPR=0 是否有 k 条件
§4.5: 现有 k 数据已充足（k=10: 0.047, k=20: 0.069）。加 1 句经验约束：
"Web-overlap annotations suggest that the majority of MMLU subjects have contamination prevalence below 50%, empirically constraining k; non-web contamination channels could affect additional domains, though the k ≤ 5 guarantee covers the documented contamination regime."

### W4: 正交性替代解释
§5.1 末尾（替换教育 DIF 对比的第 3 点以控制字数，因为该点价值最低），加：
"The near-independence admits two interpretations: (1) DIF captures a broader signal—including capability evolution and training methodology shifts—of which contamination is one component; (2) DIF and contamination detection measure distinct constructs. The base/instruct direction reversal and model-type interaction (OR = 4.75; \Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favor interpretation (1), as contamination alone cannot explain opposing DIF patterns. Either interpretation supports periodic DIF audits."

### W5: 时间粒度

5	Across six METABENCH benchmarks, 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts (${\approx}$27--29\% for MMLU; conservative bound: 13.5\%), while aggregate rankings remain preserved ($\rho \approx 0.997$). 1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
7	\label{sec:validation}
8	
9	\looseness=-1
10	A Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$ (\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).
11	
12	\subsection{Temporal DIF: 31\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}
13	\label{sec:temporal}
14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\% CI: $[29.4\%, 38.1\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$, upper bound driven by mega-family over-representation; \Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect in parallel: simple-mean DEFF (29.2\%) and cluster bootstrap (26.7\%) converge on ${\approx}$27--29\%, while the conservative weighted-mean DEFF yields 13.5\%---still ${\approx}22\times$ the random baseline (\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation}). We recommend simple-mean DEFF as the primary estimate: it converges with the cluster bootstrap and DEFF simulation, while weighted-mean DEFF over-corrects by down-weighting large families with systematically lower ICC. Under 100 random permutations within the 2023 cohort (540 vs.\ 540), the 99th percentile of item-level $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$ is 1.45---just below the ETS~C threshold of 1.5---confirming that the threshold corresponds to a per-item false-positive rate below 1\% in the LLM setting. No single family drives the signal (LOFO $|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp); graduated de-duplication confirms C\% scales with power, not redundancy (\Cref{app:dedup_k}).
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\% of 0.62\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\% (2024; 100 permutations), both ${\geq}24\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
20	
21	\looseness=-1
22	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). Domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); a Simpson's paradox confirms DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate gains (OR reverses from 1.55 to 0.73 after controlling for difficulty; \Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. C\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\ labels are not applicable.}
27	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
28	\small
29	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
30	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}

33	\midrule
34	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
35	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,600 / ${\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
36	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
37	Random split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\
38	Random split 2024 (sanity) & ${\sim}$400 / ${\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\

42	\end{table}
43	
44	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
45	\label{sec:groundtruth}
46	
47	\looseness=-1
48	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals---an empirical verification of the theoretically expected but previously untested distinction between global exposure and differential functioning (\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (bootstrap 95\% CI: $[0.94, 1.02]$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests confirm near-independence ($\phi = 0.012$, OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15], Jaccard $= 0.206$, within permutation null $[0.203, 0.222]$; $p = 0.88$). This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes. The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The two constructs---global exposure and differential functioning---are empirically orthogonal.
49	\begin{figure}[t]
50	\centering
51	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
52	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
53	\label{fig:dose_response}
54	\end{figure}
55	
56	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
57	\label{sec:structural}
58	
59	\looseness=-1
60	Two structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; cross-subject C\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\% (\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\% converges to ${\sim}$28--29\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\Cref{app:structural}).
61	
62	\begin{table}[t]
63	\centering
64	\caption{Structural controls and sensitivity checks for temporal DIF. None reduces C\% below 31\%; $\theta$-conditioning serves as a sensitivity check confirming total-score adequacy.}
65	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
66	\small
67	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
68	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
69	\toprule
70	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ C\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
71	\midrule
72	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
73	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
74	$\theta$-cond.\ (sens.) & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
75	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
76	\bottomrule
77	\end{tabular}}
78	\end{table}
79	
80	\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}
81	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
82	
83	\looseness=-1
84	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with detection-power upper bounds of $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; stylized uniform injection; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee holds for single-subject contamination ($k \leq 5$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$). Under broader contamination spanning $k = 10$ subjects (18\% of domains), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.047 \pm 0.022$; at $k = 20$, to $0.069 \pm 0.018$ (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection,app:fig_matching}). Even worst-case multi-subject inflation ($\sim$7~pp) cannot account for the observed 31\% C\% rate, which exceeds the 0.62\% random baseline by ${>}50\times$. Under DEFF adjustment, worst-case $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ at $k{=}20$ (0.069) represents up to 50\% of the 13.5\% lower bound, underscoring $k \leq 5$ in practice. Difficulty-dependent injection (non-uniform flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\ $0.51$ uniform), with distinguishable $\rho$ signatures (capability-like: $+0.77$ vs.\ contamination-like: $-0.52$; zero seed overlap), though mapping to real-data mechanisms requires further work.
85	
86	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
87	\label{sec:downstream}
88	
89	\looseness=-1
90	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks ($\rho = 0.997$, $\tau = 0.958$; flip rate $\leq 2.8\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Among the 200 most displaced models (top 3.8\%), 33.6\% of pairwise rankings reverse, concentrating among closely-ranked pairs (35.2\% at $\Delta\mathrm{rank} < 50$ vs.\ 0.0\% above 1000; \Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$). DIF-corrected scoring (stable items only) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average, with a cohort-differential of $0.29$\,pp ($d = 0.31$; individual rank displacements reach ${\pm}800$; \Cref{app:score_inflation}).
91	
92	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
93	\label{sec:gsm8k}
94	
95	\looseness=-1
96	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ vs.\ $17.9$--$18.9\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.
97

All six show substantial DIF (45--95$\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\% than procedural ones.}

117	
118	Semi-synthetic injection on GSM8K is consistent with detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory cross-benchmark signal, GSM1K contamination gaps correlate with DIF-C item accuracy ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}), but the small sample requires replication.

Difficulty-dependent injection (non-uniform flipping weighted by item difficulty) yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), with distinguishable $\\rho$ signatures (capability-like: $+0.77$ vs.\\ contamination-like: $-0.52$; zero seed overlap), though mapping to real-data mechanisms requires further work.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nStable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks ($\\rho = 0.997$, $\\tau = 0.958$; flip rate $\\leq 2.8\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Among the 200 most displaced models (top 3.8\\%), 33.6\\% of pairwise rankings reverse, concentrating among closely-ranked pairs (35.2\\% at $\\Delta\\mathrm{rank} < 50$ vs.\\ 0.0\\% above 1000; \\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ random; $p < 0.001$). DIF-corrected scoring (stable items only) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with a cohort-differential of $0.29$\\,pp ($d = 0.31$; individual rank displacements reach ${\\pm}800$; \\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks consistently higher than procedural ones (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ vs.\\ $17.9$--$18.9\\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks. C\\% = ETS~C items ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts; $\\rho$/$\\tau$ = Spearman/Kendall correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip = pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio = C\\%/Rand.\\ C\\%, with baseline varying by benchmark. All six show substantial DIF (45--95$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K is consistent with detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}). As an exploratory cross-benchmark signal, GSM1K contamination gaps correlate with DIF-C item accuracy ($r = -0.478$, $p = 0.005$, $N = 33$; \\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}), but the small sample requires replication.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TCWyd7XLLkZxsKBNawqkBs`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models), validated through a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (${\approx}$27--29\% for MMLU after non-independence corrections; conservative bound 13.5\%; random-split baseline 0.62\%). Web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of DIF flags: global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Temporal DIF does not threaten aggregate rankings ($\rho \approx 0.997$) but reveals structured item-level change---base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF patterns---and shifts DIF-corrected scores by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HYMo2tJZRjNjTBPztf5KHV`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} we find that 31\% of MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\rho \approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 849 rank positions. The incomparability is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.
5	
6	Existing approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are empirically independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}
11	\caption{Schematic illustration of global vs.\ differential contamination via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training (assuming no ceiling effect). The enrichment ratio of $\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}
12	\label{fig:global_vs_differential}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	We apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (${\approx}$27--29\% after non-independence corrections; conservative bound: 13.5\%) is not a methodological artifact (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}).
16	
17	Our contributions are:
18	
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{Temporal DIF at scale, orthogonal to contamination labels}: We establish that ${\approx}$27--29\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF across 2023--2024 model cohorts---a signal empirically independent of web-overlap contamination labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$)---revealing that global exposure and differential functioning capture complementary aspects of benchmark change. Replication across six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$. Base-vs-instruct stratification reveals mechanistically distinct patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$), providing positive evidence for mechanism separation (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).
21	
22	\item \textbf{Cross-subject matching with formal FPR guarantee}: A cross-subject matching strategy provides a constructive $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee under single-subject contamination ($k \leq 5$), solving matching-variable corruption when the benchmark itself may be compromised (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We complement this with family-level DEFF correction for non-independence and semi-synthetic validation for detection-power calibration.
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	We adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.
5	
6	\subsection{Data}
7	\label{sec:data}
8	
9	The primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.
10	
11	\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}
12	\label{sec:mhdif}
13	
14	In educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.
15	
16	The Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\citep{holland1988mh,magis2010difr} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \times 2$ contingency table:
17	
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:mh}
20	\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \,/\, T_k}{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \,/\, T_k}\,,
21	\end{equation}
22	
23	\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:
24	
25	\begin{equation}
26	\label{eq:delta}
27	\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \ln(\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}})\,.
28	\end{equation}
29	
30	The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \leq |\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. These thresholds were calibrated for human test-taker populations; we retain them as standardized reference points and verify robustness across stricter cutoffs (\Cref{sec:temporal}). Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\%. At $N = 1{,}887$ (the temporal comparison; 5{,}227 total across all cohort designs), significance ($p < 0.05$) is nearly automatically satisfied for any $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$: only 3 of 3{,}918 C items lack significance, so the dual criterion effectively reduces to a single effect-size threshold; per-item DEFF correction (\Cref{app:deff}) restores $p$-value selectivity by reducing effective $N$: under simple-mean DEFF, 269 items (6.9\%) lose significance while retaining $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 74\% of these are near-miss ($p_{\mathrm{adj}} \in [0.05, 0.10]$).
31	
32	The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\Cref{sec:structural}).
33	
34	\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces four challenges absent in educational testing. First, overlapping training corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1{,}000 replications shuffling group labels within score strata) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$~pp under cross-subject matching (\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\% (\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, bimodal ability distributions motivate quintile-based stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57-subject structure raises a multidimensionality concern~\citep{ackerman1992didmf,roussos1996multidimensionality}: domain-level ability profiles are nearly flat across cohorts (Cohen's $d$ range $= 0.12$), cross-subject and within-subject matching agree ($\kappa = 0.80$, 834 items), and cross-subject-unique C items do not concentrate in high-shift subjects (\Cref{app:structural}).
35	
36	\subsection{Cohort Designs}
37	\label{sec:cohorts}
38	
39	We construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window; (3)~\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.
40	
41	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}
42	\label{sec:injection}
43	
44	To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.
45	
46	\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = \mathrm{TPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{TPR}(\text{baseline})$ and $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = \mathrm{FPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{FPR}(\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a stylized uniform injection pattern, reported $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power; realistic contamination (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) would produce weaker signals.
47	
48	\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested.
49	
50	\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}
51	\label{prop:fpr}
52	Let $M_i^{(-s)} = \sum_{j \notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.
53	
54	When contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\leq$9\% of subjects ($k \leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).
55	
56	Empirically, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$). $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ is a constructive guarantee of the matching strategy, independent of injection realism, whereas $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ may overestimate detection power under the stylized uniform injection. $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ remains negligible for single-domain contamination ($k \leq 5$); when contamination spans many domains, within-subject matching should be preferred (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol.
57	
58	Following DIF convention~\citep{wainer1993differential}, we do not apply family-wise error correction: the dual ETS~C threshold ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ \emph{and} $p < 0.05$) guards against false positives; BH-FDR at $q = 0.05$ reclassifies only 3 of 3{,}918 C items (\Cref{app:method_details}). Diagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3, domain-specificity, DIF decomposition) and further details appear in \Cref{app:method_details}.
59	
```
